Continuous Group of Transformations
Aleks Kleyn

Abstract




[http://arxiv.org/pdf/2207.06506.pdf]

[http://arxiv.org/pdf/1601.03259v3.pdf]

[http://arxiv.org/pdf/1908.04418.pdf]

Abstract.

Let
B
be Banach algebra
and
M
be topological space.
If there exists homeomorphism

f:M\rightarrow N

of topological space
M

into convex set
N
of the space
B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}
,
then homeomorphism
f
is called
chart
of the set
M
.

The set
M
is called
simple
B
-manifold
of class
C^{k}

if for any two charts
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
,
the map
f
on commutative diagram

\textstyle{N_{1}\subseteq B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{N_{2}\subseteq B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}}

is diffeomorphism of class
C^{k}
.

Topological space
M
is called
differential
B
-manifold
of class
C^{k}

if topological space
M

is a union of simple
B
-manifolds

M_{i}
,
i\in I
,

and intersection

M_{i}\cap M_{j}

of simple
B
-manifolds

M_{i}
,
M_{j}

is also simple
B
-manifold.

Differential
B
-manifold
G

equipped with group structure
such that map

(f,g)\rightarrow fg^{-1}

is differentiable is called Lie group.
Module
T_{e}G
equipped with product

[v,w]^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it c}}}=R_{L}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it c}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it jm}}}\circ(v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}},w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}})-R_{L}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it c}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it mj}}}\circ(w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}},v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}})\in T_{e}G

is Lie algebra
g_{L}
of Lie group
G
.

Chapter 1
Preface

1.1.
Acknowledgment

I want appreciate help
of professor Gavrilchenko (Odessa university),
under whose supervision I wrote my diploma research paper
”Linear Representation of Lie Group”.
Almost 50 years have passed since then.
I am glad that at the next turn of my research
I returned to this topic,
but already in non-commutative algebra.

1.2.
Continuous Groups of Transformations

On November 8, 1975, I started my way in differential geometry.
I came to the geometry department of Odessa university
and expressed a desire to write term research paper.
Professor Sinukov said that professor Gavrilchenko
will be my adviser.

Before I started writing my term research paper,
I looked through a lot of papers and books
dedicated to Lorentz transformation
in general relativity.
I found complete chaos in understanding of this question.
As a result of wandering in this chaos,
I found definition of local reference frame,
relation between local and global reference frames,
holonomic and non-holonomic coordinates in event space.

When I finished writing my term research paper,
I suddenly felt that I put in this term research paper
everything that I knew. There was impression that
I have nothing else to write about.
This feeling scared me a lot.

I worked during a day and attended classes at university during night.
My friends who attended classes during a day
told me that professor Gavrilchenko
lectures on Lie groups and algebras.
Lectures were based on the book
[
12
].
From the first minute as I started listening to these lectures
I realized that this would be the basis of my diploma research paper.

Almost half a century has passed since then.
I am glad that I returned to this subject;
however now I will consider this subject in non-commutative algebra.

Representation of Universal Algebra

This chapter
contains definitions and theorems
which are necessary for an understanding of the text of this
book.
So the reader may read the statements from this
chapter
in process of reading the main text of the
book.

Definition 2.1.12
.

Homomorphism

2.1

Definition 2.1.13

.

Theorem 2.1.14

The equality

\displaystyle(g\circ f)(a_{1}...a_{n}\omega)

\displaystyle=g(f(a_{1}...a_{n}\omega))

\displaystyle=g(f(a_{1})...f(a_{n})\omega)

\displaystyle=g(f(a_{1}))...g(f(a_{n}))\omega

\displaystyle=(g\circ f)(a_{1})...(g\circ f)(a_{n})\omega

follows from equalities

)

Remark 2.2.5
.

We may consider a pair of maps

,

as map

Therefore, hereinafter the tuple of maps

also is called map
and we will use map

Let

Remark 2.2.6
.

Consider morphism of representations

(r_{1}:A_{1}\rightarrow B_{1},\ r_{2}:A_{2}\rightarrow B_{2})

We denote elements of the set
B_{1}
by letter using pattern
b\in B_{1}
.
However if we want to show that
b
is image of element

a\in A_{1}
,

we use notation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r_{1}(a)}
.
Thus equation

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r_{1}(a)}=r_{1}(a)

means that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r_{1}(a)}
(in left part of equation)
is image

a\in A_{1}

(in right part of equation).
Using such considerations, we denote
element of set
B_{2}
as
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.8}r_{2}(m)}
.
We will follow this convention when we consider correspondences
between homomorphisms of
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
and maps between sets
where we defined corresponding representations.

∎

Definition 2.3.8

Theorem 2.3.9

If the set

Remark 2.3.10
.

The proof of the theorem

2.3.9

gives us effective method for constructing the quasi-basis of the representation
f
.
Choosing an arbitrary generating set, step by step, we
remove from set those elements which have coordinates
relative to other elements of the set.
If the generating set of the representation is infinite,
then this construction may not have the last step.
If the representation has finite generating set,
then we need a finite number of steps to construct a quasi-basis of this representation.

∎

We introduced
\Omega_{2}
-word of
x\in A_{2}
relative generating set
X

in the definition

2.3.6
.
From the theorem

2.3.9
,
it follows that if the generating set
X
is not a quasi-basis,
then a choice of
\Omega_{2}
-word relative generating set
X
is ambiguous.
However, even if the generating set
X
is an quasi-basis,
then a representation of
m\in A_{2}
in form of
\Omega_{2}
-word is ambiguous.

2.4.
Diagram of Representations of Universal Algebras

Definition 2.4.1
.

Diagram
(f,A)
of representations of universal algebras

is oriented graph such that

2.4.1.1:

the vertex of
A_{k}
,
k=1
, …,
n
, is
\Omega_{k}
-algebra;

2.4.1.2:

the edge
f_{kl}
is representation
of
\Omega_{k}
-algebra
A_{k}
in

\Omega_{l}
-algebra
A_{l}
;

We require that this graph
is connected graph and does not have loops.
Let
A_{[0]}
be set of initial vertices of the graph.
Let
A_{[k]}
be set of vertices of the graph
for which the maximum path from the initial vertices is
k
.

∎

Remark 2.4.2
.

Since different vertices of the graph
can be the same algebra,
then we denote

A=(A_{(1)}\ \ ...\ \ A_{(n)})

the set of universal algebras which are distinct.
From the equality

A=(A_{(1)}\ \ ...\ \ A_{(n)})=(A_{1}\ \ ...\ \ A_{n})

it follows that, for any index
(i)
,
there exists at least one index
i
such that

A_{(i)}=A_{i}
.

If there are two sets of sets

A=(A_{(1)}\ \ ...\ \ A_{(n)})
,

B=(B_{(1)}\ \ ...\ \ B_{(n)})
a
nd there is a map

h_{(i)}:A_{(i)}\rightarrow B_{(i)}

for an index
(i)
,
then also there is a map

h_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}

for any index
i
such that

A_{(i)}=A_{i}

and in this case
h_{i}=h_{(i)}
.

∎

Definition 2.4.3
.

Diagram
(f,A)
of representations of universal algebras
is called

commutative

when diagram meets the following requirement.
for each pair of representations

\textstyle{f_{ik}:A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{A_{k}}

\textstyle{f_{jk}:A_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{A_{k}}

the following equality is true
2.19
2.19

2.19

Metaphorically speaking,
representations
f_{ik}
and
f_{jk}

are transparent to each other.

(2.4.1)

f_{ik}(a_{i})(f_{jk}(a_{j})(a_{k}))=f_{jk}(a_{j})(f_{ik}(a_{i})(a_{k}))

∎

Consider the theorem

for the purposes of illustration of the definition

Definition 2.4.5

Chapter 3
Abelian Group

This chapter
contains definitions and theorems
which are necessary for an understanding of the text of this
book.

3.1.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let binary operation
3.1

(a,b)\in A\times A\rightarrow ab\in A

which we call product be defined on the set
A
.
The set
A
is called multiplicative monoid if

3.1.1.1:

the product is

associative

(ab)c=a(bc)

3.1.1.2:

the product has unit element
e

ea=ae=a

If product is

commutative

(3.1.1)

ab=ba

then multiplicative monoid
A
is called
commutative or Abelian.

∎

Definition 3.1.2
.

Let binary operation
3.1

(a,b)\in A\times A\rightarrow a+b\in A

which we call sum be defined on the set
A
.
The set
A
is called additive monoid if

3.1.2.1:

the sum is

associative

(a+b)+c=a+(b+c)

3.1.2.2:

the sum has zero element
0

0+a=a+0=a

If sum is

commutative

(3.1.2)

a+b=b+a

then additive monoid
A
is called
commutative or Abelian.

∎

Theorem 3.1.3
.

Unit element of multiplicative monoid
A
is unique.

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
7
]-
.
∎

Theorem 3.1.4
.

Zero element of additive monoid
A
is unique.

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
7
]-
.
∎

Remark 3.1.5
.

According to

definitions

2.1.7
,

3.1.1

and the theorem

3.1.3
,
multiplicative monoid is universal algebra
with one binary operation (multiplication)
and one
0
-ary operation (unit element).

∎

Remark 3.1.6
.

According to

definitions

2.1.7
,

3.1.2

and the theorem

3.1.4
,
additive monoid is universal algebra
with one binary operation (addition)
and one
0
-ary operation (zero element).

∎

Definition 3.1.7
.

The multiplicative monoid
3.1

A
is called multiplicative group if
for any
A
-number
x

there exists
A
-number
y

such that

xy=yx=e

A
-number
y
is called inverse for
A
-number
x
.
If product is commutative,
then multiplicative group
A
is called commutative or Abelian.

∎

Definition 3.1.8
.

The additive monoid
3.1

A
is called additive group if
for any
A
-number
x

there exists
A
-number
y

such that

x+y=y+x=0

A
-number
y
is called inverse for
A
-number
x
.
If sum is commutative,
then additive group
A
is called commutative or Abelian.

∎

Definitions of multiplicative and additive monoids
differ only in form of notation of operation.
Therefore, if choice of operation is clear from the context,
the corresponding algebra will be called monoid.
Siimilar remark is true for groups.
Definitions of multiplicative and additive groups
differ only in form of notation of operation.
Therefore, if choice of operation is clear from the context,
the corresponding algebra will be called group.
Unless otherwise stated,
we usually assume that additive group is Abelian.

Definition 3.1.9
.

Let
H
be subgroup of multiplicative group
G
.
The set

(3.1.3)

aH=\{ab:b\in H\}

is called

left coset
.
The set

Ha=\{ba:b\in H\}

is called

right coset
.

∎

Definition 3.1.10
.

Let
H
be subgroup of additive group
G
.
The set

(3.1.4)

a+H=\{a+b:b\in H\}

is called

left coset
.
The set

H+a=\{b+a:b\in H\}

is called

right coset
.

∎

3.2.
Ring

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let two binary operations
3.4
3.4

3.4

See also the definition
of ring on the page
[
1
]-83.

be defined on the set
A

•

sum

(a,b)\in A\times A\rightarrow a+b\in A

•

product

(a,b)\in A\times A\rightarrow ab\in A

3.2.1.1:

The set
A
is Abelian additive group
with respect to sum and
0
is zero element.

3.2.1.2:

The set
A
is multiplicative monoid
with respect to product.

3.2.1.3:

Product is

distributive

over sum

(3.2.1)

a(b+c)=ab+ac

(3.2.2)

(a+b)c=ac+bc

If product is commutative, then ring is called commutative.

∎

Theorem 3.2.2
.

Let
A
be a ring. Then

(3.2.3)

0a=0

for any
A
-number
a
.

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
7
]-
.
∎

Theorem 3.2.3
.

Let
A
be a ring. Then

(3.2.4)

(-a)b=-(ab)

(3.2.5)

a(-b)=-(ab)

(3.2.6)

for any
A
-numbers
a
,
b
.
If ring has unit element, then

(3.2.7)

(-1)a=-a

for any
A
-number
a
.

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
7
]-
.
∎

Definition 3.2.4
.

Let
A
be commutative ring.
If the set

A^{\prime}=A\setminus\{0\}

is multiplicative group
with respect to product,
then the set
A
is called field.

3.4.
Basis of Additive Abelian Group

In this section,
G
is additive Abelian group.

Definition 3.4.1
.

The action of ring of rational integers
Z

in additive Abelian group
G

is defined using following rules

(3.4.1)

\displaystyle 0g

\displaystyle=0

(3.4.2)

\displaystyle(n+1)g

\displaystyle=ng+g

(3.4.3)

\displaystyle(n-1)g

\displaystyle=ng-g

∎

Theorem 3.4.2
.

The action of ring of rational integers
Z

in additive Abelian group
G

defined in the definition

3.4.1

is representation.
The following equalities are true

(3.4.4)

\displaystyle 1a

\displaystyle=a

(3.4.5)

\displaystyle(nm)a

\displaystyle=n(ma)

(3.4.6)

\displaystyle(m+n)a

\displaystyle=ma+na

(3.4.7)

\displaystyle(m-n)a

\displaystyle=ma-na

(3.4.8)

\displaystyle n(a+b)

\displaystyle=na+nb

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
7
]-
.
∎

.

The set of
G
-numbers generated by the set

S=\{s_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}:\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}\}

(3.4.9)

Convention 3.4.4
.

We will use Einstein summation convention
in which repeated index (one above and one below)
implies summation with respect to repeated index.
In this case we assume that we know the set
of summation index and do not use summation symbol

g^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}s_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=\sum_{i\in I}g^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}s_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

∎

.

f:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}

holds the equality

(3.4.10)

f(na)=nf(a)

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [

]-
.

Definition 3.5.1
.

Let
S
be a set and
3.5

Ab(S,*)

be category objects of which are maps

f:S\rightarrow G

of the set
S
into multiplicative Abelian groups.
If

f:S\rightarrow G

f^{\prime}:S\rightarrow G^{\prime}

are objects of the category

Ab(S,*)

,
then morphism from
f
to
f^{\prime}

is the homomorphism of groups

g:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}

such that the diagram

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}

is commutative.

∎

Definition 3.5.2
.

S
be a set and
3.5

Ab(S,+)

be category objects of which are maps

f:S\rightarrow G

of the set
S
into additive Abelian groups.
If

f:S\rightarrow G

f^{\prime}:S\rightarrow G^{\prime}

are objects of the category

Ab(S,+)

,
then morphism from
f
to
f^{\prime}

is the homomorphism of groups

g:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}

such that the diagram

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}

is commutative.

∎

4.1.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Effective representation of commutative ring
D

in an Abelian group

Convention 4.1.6
.

We will use summation convention
in which repeated index
in linear combination
implies summation with respect to repeated index.
In this case we assume that we know the set
of summation index and do not use summation symbol

4.1.14.2:

The set

of matrices

c=(c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})

.

Proof.
Let there exist matrix

c=(c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})

such that the equality
(
4.1.19
)
is true and there exist index

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}=\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}

such that

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}){}\neq 0
.

If we assume that
D
-number

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}})

has inverse one, then the equality

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}){}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}\}}\frac{c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}){}}{c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}){}}e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}){}

follows from the equality
(
4.1.19
).
Therefore, the vector

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}})

is linear combination of other vectors of the set
\overline{\overline{e}}{}

and the set
\overline{\overline{e}}{}
is not quasi-basis.
Therefore, our assumption is false,
and
D_{(1)}
-number

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}})

does not have inverse.

Let matrices

b=(b(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})\in D^{\prime}
,

c=(c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})\in D^{\prime}
.

From equalities

b(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

it follows that

(b(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})+c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}))e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

Therefore, the set
D^{\prime}
is Abelian group.

Let matrix

c=(c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})\in D^{\prime}

and
a\in D
.
From the equality

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

it follows that

(ac(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}))e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

Therefore, Abelian group
D^{\prime}
is
D
-module.

4.3.
Tensor Product of Modules

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a category.
Let

\{B_{i},i\in I\}

be the set of objects of
\mathcal{A}
.
Object

P={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\prod_{i\in I}B_{i}}

and set of morphisms

\{f_{i}:P\rightarrow B_{i},i\in I\}

is called a

product of set of objects

\{B_{i},i\in I\}

in category
\mathcal{A}

4.7
4.7

4.7

I made definition according to [
1
], page 58.

if for any object
R

and set of morphisms

\{g_{i}:R\rightarrow B_{i},i\in I\}

there exists a unique morphism

h:R\rightarrow P

such that diagram

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\textstyle{B_{i}}
\textstyle{f_{i}\circ h=g_{i}}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{h}

is commutative for all
i\in I
.

If
|I|=n
, then we also will use notation

P={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\prod_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}B_{1}\times...\times B_{n}}

for product of set of objects

\{B_{i},i\in I\}
in
\mathcal{A}
.

∎

Theorem 4.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be category
of modules over commutative ring
D

and linear maps be morphisms of the category
\mathcal{M}
.
There exists product in the category
\mathcal{M}

and the product in the category
\mathcal{M}
is called

tensor product
.

We will use notation

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}A_{1}\otimes...\otimes A_{n}}

for tensor product of
D
-modules

A_{1}
, …,
A_{n}
.

Any tensor

a\in A_{1}\otimes...\otimes A_{n}

is sum of tensors like

a_{s1}\otimes...\otimes a_{sn}
,

a_{si}\in A_{i}
.

Theorem 4.4.2
.

The multiplication in the algebra
A
is distributive over addition

(4.4.2)

(a+b)c=ac+bc

(4.4.3)

a(b+c)=ab+ac

Proof.
The statement of the theorem follows from the chain of equations

\displaystyle(a+b)c

\displaystyle=C\circ(a+b,c)=C\circ(a,c)+C\circ(b,c)=ac+bc

\displaystyle a(b+c)

\displaystyle=C\circ(a,b+c)=C\circ(a,b)+C\circ(a,c)=ab+ac

∎

Convention 4.6.6
.

We will use summation convention
in which repeated index
in linear combination
implies summation with respect to repeated index.
In this case we assume that we know the set
of summation index and do not use summation symbol

v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}}v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})

If needed to clearly show a set of indices, I will do it.

∎

Definition 4.6.7
.

be right-side vector space.

4.7.
Matrix operations

We consider matrices whose entries belong
to associative division
D
-algebra
A
.

According to the custom the product of
matrices
a
and
b
is defined as product of
rows of the matrix
a
and columns of the matrix
b
.

In non-commutative algebra, this product
is not enough to solve some problems.

Example 4.7.1
.

We represent the set of vectors

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}})=e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

of basis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}

of left vector space
V

over
D
-algebra
A

(see the definition

4.5.1

and the theorem

4.5.15
)
as
row of matrix

(4.7.1)

e=\begin{pmatrix}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

We represent coordinates

v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}})=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

of vector

\overline{v}{}=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

as column of matrix

(4.7.2)

v=\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

Therefore, we can represent the vector
\overline{v}{}

as product of matrices

(4.7.3)

v=\begin{pmatrix}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}=e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

∎

Example 4.7.2
.

We represent the set of vectors

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}})=e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

of basis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}

of right vector space
V

over
D
-algebra
A

(see the definition

4.6.1

and the theorem

4.5.15
)
as
row of matrix

(4.7.4)

e=\begin{pmatrix}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

We represent coordinates

v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}})=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

of vector

\overline{v}{}=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

as column of matrix

(4.7.5)

v=\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

However, we cannot represent the vector
\overline{v}{}

as product of matrices

(4.7.6)

v=\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \ e=\begin{pmatrix}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

because this product is not defined.

∎

From examples

4.7.1
,

4.7.2

it follows that
we cannot confine ourselves to traditional product of matrices
and we need to define two products of matrices.
To distinguish between these products we introduced
a new notation.

Definition 4.7.3
.

Let the nubmer of columns of the matrix
a
equal the number of rows of the matrix
b
.

{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product

of matrices
a
and
b
has form

(4.7.7)

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b}=\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\end{pmatrix}

(4.7.8)

{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}={a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}

(4.7.9)

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}\end{pmatrix}{}_{*}{}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}&=\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product can be expressed as product of a row of the matrix
a

over a column of the matrix
b
.

∎

Definition 4.7.4
.

Let the nubmer of rows of the matrix
a
equal the number of columns of the matrix

.

{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product

of matrices
a
and
b
has form

(4.7.10)

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}^{*}{}_{*}b}=\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}\end{pmatrix}

(4.7.11)

{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}^{*}{}_{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}={a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}

(4.7.12)

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}{}^{*}{}_{*}\begin{pmatrix}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}\end{pmatrix}&=\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}^{*}{}_{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}^{*}{}_{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}^{*}{}_{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}^{*}{}_{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product can be expressed as product of a column of the matrix
a

over a row of the matrix
b
.

4.9.
Matrix of maps

Definition 4.9.1
.

A matrix whose entries are linear maps
is called matrix of maps.

∎

Consider following operations on the set of matrices of maps

•

addition

\begin{pmatrix}f^{1}_{1}&...&f^{1}_{n}\\
...&...&...\\
f^{m}_{1}&...&f^{m}_{n}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}g^{1}_{1}&...&g^{1}_{n}\\
...&...&...\\
g^{m}_{1}&...&g^{m}_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f^{1}_{1}+g^{1}_{1}&...&f^{1}_{n}+g^{1}_{n}\\
...&...&...\\
f^{m}_{1}+g^{m}_{1}&...&f^{m}_{n}+g^{m}_{n}\end{pmatrix}

is defined entry-wise.

•

{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}
-product of matrices of maps

\begin{pmatrix}f^{1}_{1}&...&f^{1}_{k}\\
...&...&...\\
f^{m}_{1}&...&f^{m}_{k}\end{pmatrix}{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}\begin{pmatrix}g^{1}_{1}&...&g^{1}_{n}\\
...&...&...\\
g^{k}_{1}&...&g^{k}_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f^{1}_{i}\circ g^{i}_{1}&...&f^{1}_{i}\circ g^{i}_{n}\\
...&...&...\\
f^{m}_{i}\circ g^{i}_{1}&...&f^{m}_{i}\circ g^{i}_{n}\end{pmatrix}

{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}
-product

f{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}g

is defined as product of row of matrix
f

over column of matrix
g

and entry

(f{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}g)^{k}_{l}

of product is superposition of maps
f^{k}_{i}
and
g^{i}_{l}

with following sum by index
i
.

•

{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}
-product of matrix of maps
and column of
A
-numbers

\begin{pmatrix}f^{1}_{1}&...&f^{1}_{k}\\
...&...&...\\
f^{m}_{1}&...&f^{m}_{k}\end{pmatrix}{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}\begin{pmatrix}v^{1}\\
...\\
v^{k}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f^{1}_{i}\circ v^{i}\\
...\\
f^{m}_{i}\circ v^{i}\end{pmatrix}

{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}
-product

f{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}v

is defined as product of row of matrix
f

over column
v

and entry

(f{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}v)^{k}

of product is image of
A
-number
v^{i}
with respect to the map
f^{k}_{i}

with following sum by index
i
.

•

{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}
-product of matrices of maps

\begin{pmatrix}f^{1}_{1}&...&f^{1}_{n}\\
...&...&...\\
f^{k}_{1}&...&f^{k}_{n}\end{pmatrix}{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}\begin{pmatrix}g^{1}_{1}&...&g^{1}_{k}\\
...&...&...\\
g^{m}_{1}&...&g^{m}_{k}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f^{i}_{1}\circ g^{1}_{i}&...&f^{i}_{n}\circ g^{1}_{i}\\
...&...&...\\
f^{i}_{1}\circ g^{m}_{i}&...&f^{i}_{n}\circ g^{m}_{i}\end{pmatrix}

{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}
-product

f{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}g

is defined as product of column of matrix
f

over row of matrix
g

and entry

(f{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}g)^{k}_{l}

of product is superposition of maps
f^{i}_{l}
and
g^{k}_{i}

with following sum by index
i
.

•

{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}
-product of matrix of maps
and row of
A
-numbers

\begin{pmatrix}f^{1}_{1}&...&f^{1}_{n}\\
...&...&...\\
f^{k}_{1}&...&f^{k}_{n}\end{pmatrix}{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&...&v_{k}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f^{i}_{1}\circ v_{i}&...&f^{i}_{n}\circ v_{i}\end{pmatrix}

{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}
-product

f{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}v

is defined as product of column of matrix
f

over row
v

and entry

(f{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}v)_{k}

of product is image of
A
-number
v_{i}
with respect to the map
f^{i}_{k}

with following sum by index
i
.

4.10.
Linear Map of
A
-Vector Space

Definition 4.10.1
.

Let

A_{i}
,
i=1
,
2
,

be division algebra over commutative ring
D_{i}
.

Let

V_{i}
,
i=1
,
2
,

be
A_{i}
-vector space.
Morphism of diagram of representations

\textstyle{A_{1}}
\textstyle{V_{1}}
\textstyle{D_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\scriptstyle{*}

into diagram of representations

\textstyle{A_{2}}
\textstyle{V_{2}}
\textstyle{D_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\scriptstyle{*}

is called

linear map

of
A_{1}
-vector spaces
V_{1}
into

A_{2}
-vector spaces
V_{2}
.
Let us denote

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\mathcal{L}(D_{1}\rightarrow D_{2};A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2};V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})}

set of linear maps
of
A_{1}
-vector spaces module
V_{1}
into

A_{2}
-vector spaces
V_{2}
.

∎

Theorem 4.10.2
.

Let

V^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
V^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}
,

W^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
W^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

be
A
-vector spaces and

V=V^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\oplus...\oplus V^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

W=W^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\oplus...\oplus W^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

Let us represent
V
-number

v=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\oplus...\oplus v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

as column vector

(4.10.1)

v=\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

Let us represent
W
-number

w=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\oplus...\oplus w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

as column vector

(4.10.2)

w=\begin{pmatrix}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}

Then the linear map

f:V\rightarrow W

has representation as a matrix of maps

(4.10.3)

f=\begin{pmatrix}{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

such way that, if

w=f\circ v
,

then

(4.10.4)

\begin{pmatrix}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\circ v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\\
...\\
{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\circ v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\end{pmatrix}

The map

{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}:V^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\rightarrow W^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

is a linear map and is called

partial linear map
.

5.1.
Normed

-Algebra

\hyper@makecurrent

Definition 5.1.1
.

Norm
on ring

D
is a map
5.1

d\in D\rightarrow|d|\in R

which satisfies the following axioms

•

|a|\geq 0

•

|a|=0
if, and only if,
a=0

•

|ab|=|a|\ |b|

•

|a+b|\leq|a|+|b|

Ring
D
, endowed with the structure defined by a given norm on

D
, is called

normed ring
.

∎

\hyper@makecurrent

Definition 5.1.2
.

|a|
.
5.1

Norm

on

-module

which satisfies the following axioms

5.1.2.1:

\|v\|\geq 0

5.1.2.2:

\|v\|=0

if, and only if,

5.1.2.3:

5.1.2.4:

\|av\|=|a|\,\|v\|
,

,
v\in V

A

.

Definition 5.1.3
.

5.2.
Derivative of Map of Banach Algebra

Definition 5.2.1
.

The map

f:A\rightarrow B

of Banach
D
-module
A
with norm
\|a\|_{A}

into Banach
D
-module
B
with norm
\|a\|_{B}

is called

differentiable

on the set
U\subset A
,

if, at every point
x\in U
,
the increment of the map
f
can be represented as

(5.2.1)

f(x+h)-f(x)={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}d_{x}f(x)}\circ h+o(h)={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\frac{df(x)}{dx}}\circ h+o(h)

where

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\frac{df(x)}{dx}}:A\rightarrow B

is linear map of
D
-module
A
into
D
-module
B
and

o:A\rightarrow B

is such continuous map that

(5.2.2)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{\|o(h)\|_{B}}{\|h\|_{A}}=0

Linear map

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\frac{df(x)}{dx}}

is called

derivative of map

f
and the

differential
df
of the map

f
is defined by the equality

(5.2.3)

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}df}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\frac{df(x)}{dx}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}dx}

where
dx
is the differential of the argument.

∎

Remark 5.2.2
.

According to definition

5.2.1
,
the derivative of the map
f
is the map

d_{x}f=\frac{df}{dx}:x\in U\rightarrow\frac{df(x)}{dx}\in\mathcal{L}(D;A\rightarrow B)

Expressions
d_{x}f(x)
and

\displaystyle\frac{df(x)}{dx}

are different notations for the same map.

∎

Theorem 5.2.3
.

Definitions of the derivative
(
5.2.1
)
is equivalent to the definition

(5.2.4)

\displaystyle\frac{df(x)}{dx}\circ a=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0,\ t\in R}(t^{-1}(f(x+ta)-f(x)))

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
5
]-
.
∎

Theorem 5.2.4
.

Let
A
be Banach
D
-module with norm
\|a\|_{A}
.
Let

be Banach
D
-module with norm
\|b\|_{B}
.
Let

be Banach
D
-module with norm

.
Let map

be differentiable at point

and norm of the derivative of map

be finite

(5.2.5)

\left\|\frac{df(x)}{dx}\right\|=F\leq\infty

Let map

be differentiable at point

(5.2.6)

y=f(x)

and norm of the derivative of map
g

be finite

(5.2.7)

\left\|\frac{dg(y)}{dy}\right\|=G\leq\infty

The map
5.5
5.5

5.5

The notation

\displaystyle\frac{dg(f(x))}{df(x)}

means expression

\frac{dg(f(x))}{df(x)}=\left.\frac{dg(y)}{dy}\right|_{y=f(x)}

Similar remark is true for components of derivative.

(g\circ f)(x)=g(f(x))

is differentiable at point

x

(5.2.8)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{d(g\circ f)(x)}{dx}&=\frac{dg(f(x))}{df(x)}\circ\frac{df(x)}{dx}\\
\frac{d(g\circ f)(x)}{dx}\circ a&=\frac{dg(f(x))}{df(x)}\circ\frac{df(x)}{dx}\circ a\end{aligned}\right.

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
5
]-
.

Theorem 5.2.5
.

Let
B
be Banach module over commutative ring
D
.
Let
U
be open set of Banach
D
-module
A
.
Let

f:U\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(D;A^{p}\rightarrow B)

be differentiable map.
Then

(5.2.9)

\frac{df(x)\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})}{dx}\circ a_{0}=\left(\frac{df(x)}{dx}\circ a_{0}\right)\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})

Proof.
Let

a_{0}\in U
.

According to the definition

5.2.1
,
the increment of map

f
can be represented as

(5.2.10)

f(x+a_{0})-f(x)=\frac{df(x)}{dx}\circ a_{0}+o(a_{0})

where

\frac{df(x)}{dx}\in\mathcal{L}(D;A\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(D;A^{p}\rightarrow B))

and

o:A\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(D;A^{p}\rightarrow B)

is such continuous map that

(5.2.11)

\lim_{a_{0}\rightarrow 0}\frac{\|o(a_{0})\|}{\|a_{0}\|_{A}}=0

Since

,

then, for any

a_{1}
, …,
a_{p}
,

the equality

(5.2.12)

\begin{split}&\,f(x+a_{0})\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})-f(x)\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})\\
=&\,\left(\frac{df(x)}{dx}\circ a_{0}\right)\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})+o(a_{0})\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})\end{split}

follows from the equality
(
5.2.10
).
According to the theorem

5.1.14
,
the equality

(5.2.13)

\lim_{a_{0}\rightarrow 0}\frac{\|o(a_{0})\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})\|_{B}}{\|a_{0}\|_{A}}=0

follows from the equality
(
5.2.11
).
Considering the expression

as map

we get that the increment of this map
can be represented as

(5.2.14)

\begin{split}&\,f(x+a_{0})\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})-f(x)\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})\\
=&\,\left(\frac{df(x)\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})}{dx}\right)\circ a_{0}+o_{1}(a_{0})\end{split}

where

\frac{df(x)\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})}{dx}\in\mathcal{L}(D;A\rightarrow B)

and

o_{1}:A\rightarrow B

is such continuous map that

(5.2.15)

\lim_{a_{0}\rightarrow 0}\frac{\|o_{1}(a_{0})\|_{B}}{\|a_{0}\|_{A}}=0

The equality

(5.2.16)

\begin{split}&\,\left(\frac{df(x)\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})}{dx}\right)\circ a_{0}\\
=&\,\left(\frac{df(x)}{dx}\circ a_{0}\right)\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})+o(a_{0})\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})-o_{1}(a_{0})\end{split}

follows from the equalities
(
5.2.12
),
(
5.2.14
).
The equality

\lim_{a_{0}\rightarrow 0}\frac{\|o(a_{0})\circ(a_{1},...,a_{p})\|_{B}-\|o_{1}(a_{0})\|_{B}}{\|a_{0}\|_{A}}=0

follows from the equalities
(
5.2.13
),
(
5.2.15
).
The equality
(
5.2.9
)
follows from the equalities
(
5.2.16
),
(
5.2.17
).

Theorem 5.2.6
.

Let
A
be Banach
D
-module.
Let
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
,
B
be Banach
D
-algebras.
Let

h:B_{1}\times B_{2}\rightarrow B

be continuous bilinear map.
Let
f
,
g
be differentiable maps

\begin{matrix}f:A\rightarrow B_{1}&g:A\rightarrow B_{2}\end{matrix}

The map

h(f,g):A\rightarrow B

is differentiable and
the derivative satisfies to relationship

(5.2.18)

\frac{dh(f(x),g(x))}{dx}\circ a=h\left(\frac{df(x)}{dx}\circ dx,g(x)\right)+h\left(f(x),\frac{dg(x)}{dx}\circ dx\right)

(5.2.19)

\frac{d\,h(f(x),g(x))}{dx}=h\left(\frac{df(x)}{dx},g(x)\right)+h\left(f(x),\frac{dg(x)}{dx}\right)

Theorem 5.2.7
.

Let
A
be Banach
D
-module.
Let
B
,
C
be Banach
D
-algebras.
Let
f
,
g
be differentiable maps

\begin{matrix}f:A\rightarrow B&g:A\rightarrow C\end{matrix}

The derivative satisfies to relationship

\frac{df(x)\otimes g(x)}{dx}\circ a=\left(\frac{df(x)}{dx}\circ a\right)\otimes g(x)+f(x)\otimes\left(\frac{dg(x)}{dx}\circ a\right)

(5.2.20)

\frac{df(x)\otimes g(x)}{dx}=\frac{df(x)}{dx}\otimes g(x)+f(x)\otimes\frac{dg(x)}{dx}

Theorem 5.3.1

Therefore, for any

,

,

Using the theorem

5.3.1
,
we can consider the derivative of a map

Theorem 5.3.2
.

Let

A^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
A^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}
,

, …,

be Banach
D
-modules and

A=A^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\oplus...\oplus A^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

B=B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\oplus...\oplus B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

Let us represent differential

as column vector

...\\
dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

Let us represent differential

dy=dy^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\oplus...\oplus dy^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

as column vector

(5.3.1)

dy=\begin{pmatrix}dy^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
dy^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}

hen the derivative of the map

f:A\rightarrow B

f=f^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\oplus...\oplus f^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

has representation

such way that

\begin{split}y^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&=f^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}(x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}},x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}},x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}})=(x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}})^{2}+x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}\\

Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{\partial y^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}}

\displaystyle=x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}\otimes 1

\displaystyle=1\otimes x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}

\displaystyle=x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\otimes 1

(5.3.9)

\begin{split}dy^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&=f^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}(x+dx)-f^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}(x)\\

&=x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}+dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}+dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}+x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}\\

&=x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}+dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}+x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}+(x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}})^{2}+x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}+dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}\\
&-x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}-(x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}})^{2}\\
&=dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}+x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}+x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}+dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}\end{split}

∎

Theorem 5.3.5
.

Let

A^{1}
, …,
A^{n}
,

B
be Banach
D
-modules and

A=A^{1}\oplus...\oplus A^{n}

If the map

f:A\rightarrow B

has the second derivative, then the second derivative has the following form

\frac{d^{2}f}{dx^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}}\circ(h_{1},h_{2})={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}}\circ(h_{1}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}},h_{2}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}})

where

h_{1}=h_{1}^{1}\oplus...\oplus h_{1}^{n}

h_{2}=h_{2}^{1}\oplus...\oplus h_{2}^{n}

and we define

partial derivative of second order

by the equality

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}

Theorem 5.3.6
.

Let

A^{1}
, …,
A^{n}
,

B
be Banach
D
-modules and

A=A^{1}\oplus...\oplus A^{n}

Let derivatives of map

f:A\rightarrow B

are continuous and differentiable on the set
U\subset A
.
Let partial derivatives of second order
are continuous on the set
U\subset A
.
Then on the set
U
partial derivatives
satisfy equality

(5.3.10)

\frac{\partial^{2}f(x)}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}=\frac{\partial^{2}f(x)}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}

Chapter 6
Differential Geometry

6.1.
Differential Manifold over Algebra

Definition 6.1.1
.

Let
B
be Banach algebra
and
M
be topological space.
If there exists homeomorphism

f:M\rightarrow N

of topological space
M

into convex set
N
of the space
B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}
,
then homeomorphism
f
is called
chart
of the set
M
.

∎

Remark 6.1.2
.

If the map

f:M\rightarrow N\subseteq B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

is chart of the set
M
,
then for any point
m\in M

there exists tuple

(m^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}},...,m^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}})

of
B
-numbers
which uniquely defines the point
m
.

∎

Remark 6.1.3
.

If there exist two charts

f_{1}:M\rightarrow N_{1}\subseteq B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

f_{2}:M\rightarrow N_{2}\subseteq B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

then the map
f
on commutative diagram

(6.1.1)

\textstyle{N_{1}\subseteq B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{N_{2}\subseteq B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}}

is homeomorphism.
We can represent homeomorphism
f

as system of equalities

(6.1.2)

\displaystyle y^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}

\displaystyle=f^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}(x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}},...x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}})

\displaystyle...

\displaystyle y^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

\displaystyle=f^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}(x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}},...x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}})

where

(x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}},...,x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}})\in N_{1}
,

(y^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}},...,y^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}})\in N_{2}
.

If maps

f^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
f^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

have derivative of order
k
,
then the map
f
is called

diffeomorphism

of class
C^{k}
.

∎

Definition 6.1.4
.

Let
B
be Banach algebra.
The set
M
is called

simple
B
-manifold

of class
C^{k}

if for any two charts
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
,
the map
f
on commutative diagram

(
6.1.1
)
\textstyle{N_{1}\subseteq B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{N_{2}\subseteq B^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}}

is diffeomorphism of class
C^{k}
.

∎

Definition 6.1.5
.

Topological space
M
is called
differential
B
-manifold
of class
C^{k}

if topological space
M

is a union of simple
B
-manifolds

M_{i}
,
i\in I
,

and intersection

M_{i}\cap M_{j}

of simple
B
-manifolds

M_{i}
,
M_{j}

is also simple
B
-manifold.

∎

Example 6.1.6
.

Two-dimensional sphere is
the simplest example of non-trivial differential manifold.
There is no homeomorphism mapping sphere to a plane.
However, we can consider sphere as union of two hemispheres;
for instance, union of northern hemisphere and southern.
We assume that both hemispheres
have a common strip along the equator.
Stereographic projection

(6.1.3)

\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S}

is diffeomorphism of hemisphere to plane.

∎

6.2.
Tangent Space

In commutative algebra
we define vector which is tangent to manifold
according to law of transformation of coordinates of vector
when we change basis.
In non-commutative algebra
this definition is unsatisfactory because
in non-commutative algebra
there is difference between homomorphism
and linear map.

In commutative algebra
we also identify vector and differentiation.
This definition is equivalent to the one discussed above.
We can consider a similar definition
in non-commutative algebra.

Definition 6.2.1
.

Let
M
be differential
B
-manifold.
Let
U
be chart of manifold
M
such that
x\in U
.
Left
B
-module
T_{x}M

generated by the set of partial derivatives

(6.2.1)

\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}}\ \ \ \,...\ \ \ \,\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}}

at point
x\in M

is called tangent space.
The set of partial derivatives
(
6.2.1
)
form basis of tangent space
T_{x}M

and we call this basis the coordinate basis.

∎

In commutative algebra linear combination
of differentiations is differentiation.
In non-commutative algebra
this statement is false as follows from the equality

(6.2.2)

\displaystyle a\frac{\partial fg}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}

\displaystyle=a\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}g+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}af}\frac{\partial g}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}

\displaystyle\neq a\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}g+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}fa}\frac{\partial g}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}

However we elements of tangent space
call vectors or differentiations.

Definition 6.2.2
.

If we at every point
x
of chart
U
choose a basis

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{x}
of tangent space
T_{x}M

and coordinates of basis

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{x}
with respect to coordinate basis
are differentiable map of point
x
,
then set of bases

(6.2.3)

\overline{\overline{e}}{}=\{\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{x}{}:x\in U\}

is called reference frame of manifold
M
in chart
U

or just

reference frame
.

∎

Definition 6.2.3
.

If at every point
x\in U
basis

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{x}
is coordinate basis,
then reference frame

\overline{\overline{e}}
is called

coordinate reference frame
.

∎

Definition 6.2.4
.

Let basis

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{x}
have coordinates
{e}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}
with respect
to coordinate basis

(6.2.4)

e_{x\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}={e}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}\circ\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}

Anholonomity object

is defined by equality

(6.2.5)

{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it kl}}}=\frac{\partial{e}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}-\frac{\partial{e}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}

If

(6.2.6)

{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it kl}}}=0\otimes 0

then there exists chart
U
of manifold
M
,
in which the reference frame

\overline{\overline{e}}
is coordinate reference frame.

∎

6.3.
Lie Derivative

Definition 6.3.1
.

The map

(6.3.1)

v:x\in M\rightarrow T_{x}M

is called vector field on manifold
M
.

∎

Vector field

(6.3.2)

\overline{v}{}=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}

on manifold
generates infinitesimal transformation

(6.3.3)

x^{\prime\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}=x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}+\epsilon v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}

where
\epsilon\in R
.
Infinitesimal transformation
(
6.3.3
),
generates linear map

(6.3.4)

\frac{\partial x^{\prime\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}={\delta}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}(1\otimes 1)+\epsilon\frac{\partial v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}

of tangent space
T_{x}M

into tangent space
T_{x+\epsilon v}M
.
Let

(6.3.5)

\overline{w}{}=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}

be another vector field.
From the equality
(
6.3.4
)
it follows that vector field
w
is transformed according to the equality

(6.3.6)

w^{\prime\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}(x^{\prime})=\frac{\partial x^{\prime\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}\circ w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}(x)+\epsilon\frac{\partial v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}\circ w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}

Since vector field
w
is differentiable,
then the equality

(6.3.7)

w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}(x^{\prime})=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}(x)+\epsilon\frac{\partial w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}\circ v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}

follows from the definition of derivative.
According to definition of Lie derivative we have

(6.3.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon}(w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}(x^{\prime})-w^{\prime\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}(x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left(\epsilon\frac{\partial w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}\circ v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}-\epsilon\frac{\partial v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}\circ w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}\right)

The equality

(6.3.9)

\mathcal{L}_{v}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}=\frac{\partial w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}\circ v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}-\frac{\partial v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}\circ w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}

follows from the equality
(
6.3.8
).

6.4.
Commutator of Vector Fields

Commutator of vector fields

(6.4.1)

\overline{v}{}=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}

(6.4.2)

\overline{w}{}=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}

is defined by the equality

(6.4.3)

\displaystyle[\overline{v}{},\overline{w}{}]

\displaystyle=\left(v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}\right)\circ\left(w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}\right)-\left(w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}\right)\circ\left(v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\frac{\partial w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}\circ\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}+v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}-w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\frac{\partial v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}\circ\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}-w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}

The expression

v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}-w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}-w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}

vanishes if

(6.4.4)

v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}

The equality

(6.4.5)

[\overline{v}{},\overline{w}{}]=\left(v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\frac{\partial w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}-w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\frac{\partial v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}}\right)\circ\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}}

follows from equalities

(
6.4.3
),
(
6.4.4
).

Therefore, commutator of vector fields
(
6.4.5
)
similar to the Lie derivative

(
6.3.9
)
\mathcal{L}_{v}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}=\frac{\partial w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}\circ v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}-\frac{\partial v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial x^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}\circ w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}

However the order of factors is different
and commutator of vector fields
is not vector field.

a_{3}=\varphi(a_{1},a_{2})=a_{1}a_{2}

Let map

7.1

(

Derivative of left shift

(7.1.4)

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}A_{L}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}(b,a)}=\frac{\partial(ba)^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}

A_{L}(a,b)=\begin{pmatrix}A_{L}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}(a,b)&...&A_{L}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}(a,b)\\
...&...&...\\

If algebra
A

A_{L}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}(a,b)\in A

If algebra
A

.

\displaystyle A_{L}(b,ca){}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}A_{L}(c,a)

\displaystyle=

)
and chain rule.
The equality
(

(

Lemma 7.1.6
.

(7.1.12)

A_{L}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}(e,a)={\delta}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}\otimes{\delta}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}

Proof.
The equality

(7.1.13)

A_{L}(b,a){}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}A_{L}(e,a)=A_{L}(b,a)

follows from the equality

ea=a

and the equality

(
7.1.6
)
\begin{aligned} &\,A_{L}(b,ca){}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}A_{L}(c,a)\\
=&\,A_{L}(bc,a)\end{aligned}

if we assume

c=e
.

According to convention

7.1.2

the matrix

A_{L}(b,a)

is non-singular.
Therefore, the equality
(
7.1.12
)
follows from the equality
(
7.1.13
).

∎

Lemma 7.1.7
.

(7.1.14)

A_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}(a,e)={\delta}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}\otimes{\delta}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}

Proof.

(7.1.15)

A_{R}(a,c){}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}A_{R}(a,e)=A_{R}(a,c)

follows from the equality

be=b

and the equality

(
7.1.9
)
\begin{aligned} &\,A_{R}(ab,c){}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}A_{R}(a,b)\\
=&\,A_{R}(a,bc)\end{aligned}

if we assume

b=e
.

According to convention

7.1.3

the matrix

A_{R}(a,c)

is non-singular.
Therefore, the equality
(
7.1.14
)
follows from the equality
(
7.1.15
).

∎

if we assume

.

follows from the equality

(
7.1.12
)
A_{L}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}(e,a)={\delta}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}\otimes{\delta}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}

and from the equality
(
7.1.17
).

(
7.1.16
)

(
7.1.18
).

∎

7.2.
Right Shift

Summary of Results 7.2.1
.

For the right shift

(
7.2.1
)
c=ab

the system of differential equations

(
7.1.42
)
\displaystyle\frac{\partial(ab)^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}=\psi_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it t}}}(ab)\circ\lambda_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it t}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}(a)

gets form

(
7.2.2
)
\displaystyle\frac{\partial c^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}}{\partial a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}}=\psi_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it t}}}(c)\circ\lambda_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it t}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it l}}}(a)

The equality

(
7.2.5
)
\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\lambda_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it c}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}(c)\circ\frac{\partial\psi_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it t}}}(c)}{\partial c^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}}\circ\psi_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it q}}}(c)\circ(\lambda_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it q}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it b}}}(a)\circ\lambda_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it t}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it a}}}(a)-\lambda_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it q}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it a}}}(a)\circ\lambda_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it t}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it b}}}(a))\\
=&\frac{\partial\lambda_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it c}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it b}}}(a)}{\partial a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it a}}}}-\frac{\partial\lambda_{R}{}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it c}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it a}}}(a)}{\partial a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it b}}}}\end{aligned}

follows from
condition of integrability of the system of differential equations
(
7.2.2
).

gets form
